Unified Reference

KHANARY Atomic Master Guide

v2026.09 21 guides merged Canonical

Single authoritative reference for the KHANARY stack: ASX Atomic HTML, XJSON, SCX compression, .asx shell format, XCFE control flow, K'uhul phase system, SCXQ2 runtime, and the three-file OS pattern. All 21 prior guides are superseded by this document.

Revelation. The stack now formally declares what was already emerging in practice: token->Book addressing, folds as bounded distributed semantic context, incidence topology B separated from pattern-strength W_E, and a dedicated distributional similarity channel for context overlap.

1 · Overview & Philosophy

Core Axiom

ASX = XCFE = XJSON = KUHUL = AST
All blocks are ATOMIC_BLOCK

Holotape Metaphor

The .asx file is a holotape — a self-contained JSON cartridge describing an entire application. The ASX Engine is the player. You write pure JSON; the engine renders, routes, reacts, and executes. No build step. No framework. No compiler.

Technology Relationships

⍟ ASX — Atomic Shell ⟁ K'UHUL — Phase Law ⧉ XJSON — Universal AST ⌬ SCX — Compression ⎊ KLH — Mesh Router
LayerRoleFormat
ASXApplication shell and block renderer.asx JSON, .asxtape
XJSONUniversal AST: HTML+CSS+JS+State+Agents as onetape.xjson, xjson:1.0
SCXSymbolic compression for CSS/HTML/K'uhul⟁-prefixed symbol strings
XCFEControl-flow engine (@if/@then/@else)Embedded in XJSON/ASX blocks
K'UHULPhase execution law (Pop→Wo→Yax→Sek→Ch'en→Xul).kuhul, .khl, .kson
SCXQ2Weight bytecode / cipher runtime.scxq2, .scxqdds
KLHMesh router + backend name resolverkuhul_route.khl
MX2LMLanguage model coordinator agentmx2lm agent config

Rule of Three — The Minimal OS

Every ASX application needs exactly three files:

index.html

Shell + runtime. Loads the tape and boots the app. ≤40KB target.

sw.js

Service worker. Offline cache, TAPE_HEARTBEAT, background sync.

manifest.json

PWA manifest. App name, icons, display mode, start URL.

This three-file structure is a complete, installable, offline-capable web OS. Everything else (agents, tapes, shards) layers on top.

Design principle: Resist the urge to invent ad-hoc CSS. Encode new atoms as tokens — add to the :root block and use the attribute system.

3 · ASX Atomic Blocks Language

Mental Model (3 + 1 Layers)

Layer 1 — HTML

Canvas. Semantic structure with atomic attribute tokens applied directly.

Layer 2 — Atomic CSS

Token system. All styling via var(--asx-*) and [attr] selectors.

Layer 3 — ASX Blocks

JSON payloads. Components declared as data, rendered by the ASX runtime.

Layer 4 — XCFE Blocks

Control flow. @if/@then/@else branching logic serialized as JSON.

Full ASX Block Shape

{
  "type":      "asx-block",
  "id":        "block:user-profile:abc123",
  "component": "UserProfile",
  "encrypted": false,
  "signature": "asx-sha256-...",
  "props":     { "username": "canna", "tier": "crown" },
  "layout":    { "gridArea": "sidebar", "priority": 2 }
}

XCFE-lite Control Block

{
  "@if":   "props.balance >= 1",
  "@then": { "type":"asx-block", "component":"TokenBalance", "props":{ "balance": 2840 } },
  "@else": { "type":"asx-block", "component":"EmptyState",   "props":{ "label":"No balance" } }
}

ASX Block Glyphs (Studio/Canvas)

GlyphBlock TypeSymbolic Codec
⎔Container"⟁":"system/backend"
▦Grid"⧉":"data/json"
◰Panel"⌬":"engine/processor"
⬡Header"⍟":"atomic/core"
◷Sidebar"⎊":"network/mesh"
▣Content"⏣":"storage/memory"
◫Footer"⌖":"interface/ui"
◈Hero"⯎":"execution/runtime"

ASX Block Runtime V4 (JavaScript)

window.ASX = {
  Components: {},
  env: {},

  registerComponent(name, renderFn) {
    this.Components[name] = renderFn;
  },

  evalCondition(expr, ctx) {
    try { return new Function("ctx", "with(ctx){ return !!(" + expr + "); }")(ctx); }
    catch(e) { return false; }
  },

  processNode(node, ctx = {}) {
    if (Array.isArray(node)) return node.map(n => this.processNode(n, ctx)).join("");
    if (node["@if"] !== undefined) {
      const cond = this.evalCondition(node["@if"], ctx);
      return this.processNode(cond ? node["@then"] : node["@else"], ctx);
    }
    return this.renderBlock(node, ctx);
  },

  renderBlock(block, ctx = {}) {
    if (!block || !block.component) return "";
    const fn = this.Components[block.component];
    if (!fn) return ``;
    return fn(block.props || {}, ctx);
  },

  renderBlocks(blocks, containerId) {
    const el = document.getElementById(containerId);
    if (!el) return;
    el.innerHTML = this.processNode(blocks);
  },

  init() { /* boot hooks */ }
};

TAPE_HEARTBEAT Pattern (Service Worker)

// Register tape with K'uhul process table via service worker
const chan = new MessageChannel();
navigator.serviceWorker.controller.postMessage(
  { type: "TAPE_HEARTBEAT", tape: TAPE_ID, url: location.href, time: Date.now() },
  [chan.port2]
);

4 · XJSON Specification

XJSON is a universal AST-JSON language capable of representing any: HTML, CSS, Logic (JS/PY/PHP), State, Routing, Components, Templates, SCX blocks, Compression, Agents, and OS-level layout — all in one unified format.

Document Root

{ "xjson": "1.0", "nodes": [] }

Node Schema

{
  "type":     "element|style|logic|component|route|state|agent|tape|flashram|control",
  "id":       "unique.node.id",
  "meta":     { "label": "...", "version": "1.0" },
  "props":    {},
  "children": []
}

Node Types

TypePurposeKey props
elementDOM element (div, button, input, etc.)tag, attrs, children
styleCSS rules + SCX token maptokens, raw[]
logicEvent-driven JS actionslang, trigger, actions[]
componentReusable named subtreename, props, template
routeClient-side route definitionpath, component, guards[]
stateApplication state declarationinitial, schema
agentMX2 / AI agent configurationid, role, skills[], endpoints
tapeHolotape bundle (mini OS unit)id, label, version, nodes[]
flashramSnapshot / session statesnapshot{}
controlXCFE @if/@then/@else block@if, @then, @else

Style Node with SCX Manifest

The style node's tokens field mirrors the css.manifest.json format exactly — each key maps to an array of CSS strings or other SCX symbol keys.

{
  "type": "style",
  "id":   "core.scx",
  "tokens": {
    "⟁F":       ["display:flex"],
    "⟁Fcol":    ["display:flex", "flex-direction:column"],
    "⟁Acenter": ["align-items:center"],
    "⟁BRlg":    ["border-radius:12px"],
    "⟁BGpanel": ["background:var(--asx-bg-panel)"],
    "⟁BORDER":  ["border:1px solid rgba(148,163,184,0.3)"],
    "⟁PANEL":   ["⟁BGpanel", "⟁BRlg", "padding:20px", "⟁BORDER"],
    "⟁CARD":    ["⟁PANEL", "box-shadow:0 8px 32px rgba(0,0,0,0.2)"]
  },
  "raw": ["body[scx~='⟁BODY']{ min-height:100vh; }"]
}

Logic Node with Event Trigger

{
  "type":    "logic",
  "id":      "prime.incrementScore",
  "lang":    "js",
  "trigger": { "event": "click", "selector": "#btnStateInc" },
  "actions": [
    { "kind": "state.increment", "path": "user.score", "value": 5 }
  ]
}

Agent Node

{
  "type":      "agent",
  "id":        "mx2lm",
  "role":      "coordinator",
  "skills":    ["routing", "logic", "intent"],
  "endpoints": {
    "infer": "/api/mx2lm/infer",
    "train": "/api/mx2lm/train"
  }
}

Flash-RAM Snapshot

{ "type": "flashram", "snapshot": { "theme": "grid", "score": 120, "route": "game" } }

SCX Block Vocabulary

BlockHTML Element / Responsibility
⟁HTMLRoot <html> container
⟁HEAD<head> — meta, title, links
⟁BODYTop-level <body> frame
⟁HEADERPage header region (brand, nav)
⟁MAINMain content layout grid
⟁FOOTERFooter / status bar area
⟁CARDGeneric panel/card wrapper

⟁FORMStructured form layout
⟁TABLETable styling block
⟁PAGEFull-page layout wrapper
⟁GRIDCSS grid container
⟁DIVGeneric atomic div

Code → AST → XJSON Transformation

The XJSON builder engine transforms any source format:

Input: HTML / CSS / JS / Config
  ↓
Abstract Syntax Tree (AST)
  ↓
XJSON nodes (element / style / logic / state / ...)
  ↓
Output bundle:
  index.html        ← shell
  ui.xjson          ← UI tree
  style.xjson       ← style + SCX token map
  logic.xjson       ← event handlers + state ops
  runtime.js        ← XCFE engine embedded
  agents/           ← agent configurations
  flashram/         ← state snapshots

ABNF Grammar

xjson  = object
node   = element / style / logic / agent / route / component / control
object = "{" members "}"
array  = "[" nodes "]"
nodes  = node *("," node)

5 · SCX Compression & the Manifest System

SCX (Symbolic Compression eXtension) operates at two levels. Symbolic compression is manifest-driven shorthand — a key replaces its expanded atomic definition everywhere it is used; instances carry the key, the manifest holds the definition once. Semantic compression is meaning-preserving — each ⟁ symbol is a stable KAST identity that survives style changes, allowing the fold layer to encode, replay, and train on intent rather than values.

SCX compression law: compression begins at definition, not after serialization. The manifest establishes a symbolic namespace before any instance is written. An instance carries only the key — ⟁CARD — not the expanded definition. The runtime resolves the key through a deterministic manifest scope chain. The definition exists once, within its authority scope; every reference to it is free.
A manifest is independent of the runtime. The runtime mounts manifests — it does not generate or infer them. This is precisely why every key must be explicitly declared: the manifest is the authority on what a symbol means; the runtime is the authority on how to execute it. The two concerns are separated at the manifest boundary. A manifest declared once can be mounted into any conforming runtime — ASX browser engine, KXML inference graph, XCFE executor — without modification. Portability is a direct consequence of independence.

The underlying mechanism — key → array — is universal. A manifest can key anything with a stable resolvable identity: CSS atomics, K'uhul opcodes, words, reasoning operators, micronauts, tracks, executors, tensors, schemas, routes, or entire compositions. The array declares what that identity is composed of. Instances in any of these domains carry compact keys instead of expanded definitions.

{
  "atoms": {
    "⟁PANEL": ["background:#091310", "border:1px solid #1c5f4c"],
    "⟁CARD":  ["⟁PANEL", "box-shadow:0 8px 32px rgba(0,0,0,0.2)"]
  },

  "reasoning": {
    "⟁WHY": ["eliza.intent.why", "XCFE:INFER", "XCFE:COUNTEREXAMPLE", "fold:Sek"]
  },

  "micronauts": {
    "⟁CODE": ["CODE-µ", "track:code", "executor:code"]
  }
}

Three completely different expansion structures — CSS composition, reasoning machinery, micronaut/track composition — share the same compact handle format. ⟁CARD expands into CSS; ⟁WHY expands into AIML/XCFE operators; ⟁CODE expands into a micronaut tree. The instance carries one token; the manifest resolves the full depth.

Not Word → Word. Symbol → Book.

This is the conceptual jump that separates SCX from dictionary compression. Traditional compression maps a word to a shorter symbol — the symbol is still just a label, just smaller. SCX maps a symbol to a semantic structure — the symbol becomes a stable handle into an entire book of relationships and behavior.

MechanismWhat the symbol stands for
Dictionary compressionA shorter label — same kind of thing, fewer bytes
SCXA book: words, patterns, operators, phase assignments, tracks, relational mappings — an entire semantic domain compressed into one identity

⟁CAUSE is not shorthand for the word "cause." It is a stable identity that stands for a semantic book:

⟁CAUSE
   │
   ├── words        why, because, cause, therefore
   ├── AIML         causal patterns
   ├── ELIZA        causal reflection operators
   ├── XCFE         INFER · DECOMPOSE · COUNTEREXAMPLE
   ├── K'uhul       fold:Sek
   ├── tracks       causal-reasoning
   └── relations    cause→effect · premise→consequence · evidence→conclusion

Yet the runtime carries only: ⟁CAUSE

The book does not have to be opened. The manifest tells the system the book exists and where its semantic contents resolve. Each consumer requests only the page it needs — Yax requests capability metadata, XCFE requests its operators, ELIZA requests its patterns, K'uhul requests the phase assignment. The rest of the book is never materialised.

1 symbol ↔ N semantic atoms ↔ N relationships ↔ N behaviors
symbol → book  ·  consumer → only the required page

This makes the manifest an SCX semantic index — not a lookup table for shorter strings, but an index into books of semantic content that are lazily opened per consumer. Calling SCX a "compression algorithm" undersells it: it is a semantic addressing system where compression is a side effect of identity.

And it explains why atomics matter: you are not scanning serialised output for repeated substrings after the fact. You are deciding, before the system runs, which tiny identities are capable of standing for entire books.

In graph terms: the book is the node's neighborhood — the set of all vertices directly reachable from it. The manifest array is the adjacency list. ⟁CAUSE is a node; its array declares its neighbors: word nodes, AIML nodes, XCFE operator nodes, a K'uhul phase node, track nodes, relation nodes. The manifest is therefore a graph adjacency index — a flat declaration of every node's neighborhood before the graph is traversed. A horizontal fold across two manifest nodes is a same-depth traversal of two neighborhoods simultaneously, at zero phase cost.

One Declaration, Three Interpretations

This is the point where SCX compression, graph semantics, and K'uhul execution stop looking like three unrelated mechanisms. They share the same atomic substrate and operate on different dimensions of the same declaration.

Manifest(k) = N(k) = Book(k)
One declaration. Three interpretations. No redundant copies. 
{
  "⟁CAUSE": ["word:why", "word:because", "XCFE:INFER", "XCFE:COUNTEREXAMPLE", "fold:Sek"]
}

SCX      ⟁CAUSE = Book(semantic payload)          — symbolic address for the whole structure
Graph    N(⟁CAUSE) = {why, because, INFER, COUNTEREXAMPLE, Sek}  — adjacency list
Manifest ⟁CAUSE → [adjacent atomic identities]    — declared neighborhood

There are not three copies of the information. There is one declaration read three ways by three different consumers. The manifest does not serve SCX first and then export to the graph layer — the array is the adjacency list is the book contents, simultaneously.

Recursive Compression

Because members of N(k) can themselves be manifest keys, the compression recurses:

k  → {k₁, k₂, k₃}
k₁ → N(k₁)
k₂ → N(k₂)
k₃ → N(k₃)

                ⟁CAUSE
             /     │      \
          ⟁WHY   ⟁INFER   ⟁SEK
          / \       │        │
        ...  ...   ...      ...

The compressed symbol is not shorthand for a flat blob. It is an entry point into a recursively addressable semantic neighborhood. Compression value scales with how much reusable structure is reachable from the key:

CompressionValue(k)  ∝  reusable structure reachable from k
                         ─────────────────────────────────────
                              cost of representing k

A badly designed manifest produces weak compression because its keys do not capture reusable neighborhoods. A properly designed manifest makes one symbol stand for an enormous recursively shared structure.

LawStatement
SCX Book ≡ Manifest Neighborhood ≡ Adjacency DeclarationThe three readings are the same object
Manifest key = compressed semantic addressA key is an entry point, not a label
Manifest array = declared neighborhoodThe array is the adjacency list
Horizontal fold = within-phase semantic traversalΔθ = 0 — no phase transition occurs
Vertical fold = K'uhul phase transitionΔθ = π/3 — phase authority is required
Graph reachability does not grant execution authorityTraversing a neighborhood edge does not trigger a phase transition — XCFE authorization is required

Symbolic Layer

Size reduction. The key replaces its expanded definition at every use-site. Composition compounds this — ⟁APP can represent an enormous expansion tree that is resolved lazily, only as far as the consumer needs.

Semantic Layer

Identity preservation. A ⟁ symbol is bound to a stable KAST node. ⟁CARD means "this is a card" even after its CSS array changes — agents train on the identity, not the current values.

Resolution Layer

Scope chain. A key is resolved through the manifests mounted into the current context — instance → local → package → runtime → system. The definition need not be repeated at the instance level.

89%
CSS savings
83%
HTML savings
87%
K'uhul savings

Symbolic Compression — Manifest Format (key → array)

Every symbol is declared as "key": [ ...declarations or symbol refs... ]. Array elements are either raw CSS declaration strings or references to other SCX keys (enabling composition).

{
  "⟁F":        ["display:flex"],
  "⟁Frow":     ["display:flex", "flex-direction:row"],
  "⟁Fcol":     ["display:flex", "flex-direction:column"],
  "⟁Acenter":  ["align-items:center"],
  "⟁Astart":   ["align-items:flex-start"],
  "⟁Jcenter":  ["justify-content:center"],
  "⟁Jbetween": ["justify-content:space-between"],
  "⟁Pmd":      ["padding:16px"],
  "⟁Pxl":      ["padding:20px"],
  "⟁Mmd":      ["margin:16px"],
  "⟁BRsm":     ["border-radius:8px"],
  "⟁BRlg":     ["border-radius:12px"],
  "⟁BRxl":     ["border-radius:20px"],
  "⟁BGpanel":  ["background:var(--asx-bg-panel)"],
  "⟁BGdark":   ["background:rgba(15,23,42,0.9)"],
  "⟁BORDER":   ["border:1px solid rgba(148,163,184,0.3)"],

  "⟁PANEL":    ["⟁BGpanel", "⟁BRlg", "⟁Pxl", "⟁BORDER"],
  "⟁CARD":     ["⟁PANEL", "box-shadow:0 8px 32px rgba(0,0,0,0.2)"],
  "⟁BTN":      ["padding:8px 16px", "⟁BRsm", "border:none", "cursor:pointer", "font-weight:600"]
}
Composition rule: if an array element starts with ⟁ it is a symbol reference — the runtime recursively expands it from the same manifest. Raw CSS strings are emitted directly. This means ⟁CARD inherits everything from ⟁PANEL and adds one declaration on top.

Before / After Example

Before (187 bytes).container { display:flex; flex-direction:column; align-items:center; justify-content:center; padding:20px; background:var(--asx-bg-panel); border-radius:12px; }
After — reference 5 manifest keys["⟁Fcol", "⟁Acenter", "⟁Jcenter", "⟁Pxl", "⟁PANEL"]

How the Runtime Resolves

1 · Load Manifest

Parse css.manifest.json once at boot. Keys are the SCX symbol set for this tape.

2 · Expand Array

For each array element: if it starts with ⟁, recurse into that key's array. Otherwise emit the CSS string.

3 · Apply to DOM

Resolved declarations are injected as a stylesheet or inline style. Expansion is O(depth) — typically 1–3 levels.

Resolution Scope — Resolve(k, C)

Key resolution is always context-bound. The same key can have different definitions at different scope levels, and a key reachable at a higher scope does not need to be re-declared at a lower one.

Resolve(k, C):
  search C.manifests in order:
    1. instance manifest      ← highest priority, narrowest scope
    2. local manifest         ← tape / document level
    3. package manifest       ← component library
    4. workflow manifests     ← mounted by current workflow
    5. runtime manifest       ← running ASX engine
    6. system / root manifest ← global baseline definitions

An instance only needs to carry the key — ⟁CARD — provided ⟁CARD is reachable somewhere in its resolution chain. The definition lives once at the appropriate authority level. The caller doesn't need to know which manifest holds it, which file it lives in, or how deep the expansion goes.

Definition ≠ Visibility. Defining a key in a manifest does not make it globally visible — it only makes it visible to contexts whose chain includes that manifest. A key defined in a package manifest is available to all instances using that package, but not to unrelated instances in other chains. Scope controls authority; the resolution chain controls what is in scope.
A manifest is independent of the runtime. The runtime mounts manifests — it does not generate or infer them. This is precisely why every key must be explicitly declared: the manifest is the authority on what a symbol means; the runtime is the authority on how to execute it. The two concerns are separated at the manifest boundary. A manifest declared once can be mounted into any conforming runtime — ASX browser engine, KXML inference graph, XCFE executor — without modification. Portability is a direct consequence of independence.

K'uhul Symbol Manifest

The same manifest pattern applies to K'uhul phase opcodes in a kuhul.manifest.json:

{
  "⟁Pop":   ["[Pop"],
  "⟁Wo":    ["[Wo"],
  "⟁Yax":   ["[Yax"],
  "⟁Sek":   ["→[Sek"],
  "⟁Ch'en": ["→[Ch'en"],
  "⟁Xul":   ["]"]
}

Contextual Variants

Variant suffixes override or extend a base symbol. Declare them as separate keys in the manifest:

{
  "⟁BTN":          ["padding:8px 16px", "⟁BRsm", "border:none", "cursor:pointer", "font-weight:600"],
  "⟁BTN[primary]": ["⟁BTN", "background:var(--asx-primary)", "color:var(--asx-bg)"],
  "⟁BTN[danger]":  ["⟁BTN", "background:var(--asx-danger)",  "color:var(--asx-bg)"]
}

Manifest as Node Registry — Direct Key Access

A manifest is not a config file you parse sequentially. It is a live node registry — you address any key directly by name and receive its array immediately. Think of it as a drive-thru window: you pull up to the exact key you need and get what you ordered, no traversal, no parsing, O(1) access. Each key is a node.

manifest.get("⟁CARD")   →  ["⟁PANEL", "box-shadow:0 8px 32px rgba(0,0,0,0.2)"]
manifest.get("coordinator") → ["eliza", "coder", "routing", "⟁Ch'en"]
manifest.get("hello")     →  ["greeting", "intent:salutation", "⟁Pop"]

This is the same access pattern as a KAST node lookup, a DOM node by ID, or a hash table entry. The manifest is the graph at the vocabulary level.

Manifest Key vs Graph Node — a necessary distinction

Manifest keys and graph nodes are related but not the same thing. Collapsing the two causes confusion when a key resolves to different target types.

ConceptWhat it isPrimary property
Manifest keyA symbolic address — a name that the resolution chain can look upIdentity / address
Graph nodeA semantic entity with state, edges, and fold-phase membershipSemantic entity / state

A manifest key can resolve to a graph node — but it can also resolve to a flat CSS array, a reasoning operator list, a micronaut capability tree, or any other expansion. Use @kind and @ref to declare what type of entity a key resolves into:

{
  "⟁WHY":  { "@kind": "semantic",  "@ref": "graph://reasoning/why"  },
  "⟁CARD": { "@kind": "atomic.css","@ref": "css://atoms/card"        }
}

⟁WHY resolves into semantic graph topology — it has fold-phase membership, gravity affinity, and edges to other reasoning nodes. ⟁CARD resolves into a CSS expansion — it has no graph topology, only an array of declarations. Both are manifest keys; only the first is necessarily a graph node. The distinction saves significant complexity when the resolution chain reaches a key and needs to know what to do with it.

Word Manifests — ELIZA / AIML Vocabulary

Words are valid manifest keys. Defining a word in the manifest gives it a semantic array — a list of intents, phases, XCFE conditions, or other word references. ELIZA and AIML pattern matchers resolve input words by direct manifest lookup: the word comes in, the array comes back, the array drives the response.

This is substantially different from tokenization:

MechanismResolutionWhat the ID carries
Tokenizer "why" → 4312 Which token — a position in a vocabulary table. No semantic content.
SCX manifest "why" → ⟁CAUSE → {patterns, relationships, XCFE operators, tracks, fold:Sek} What semantic machinery that identity resolves into — patterns, reasoning operators, phase affinity, XCFE conditions.

A token ID mostly says which word. A manifest key says what the word activates — which semantic machinery, which reasoning operators, which fold phase, which XCFE branch tree. The compressed symbolic address and the semantic definition are both carried by the single key.

{
  "hello":     ["greeting", "intent:salutation", "⟁Pop"],
  "help":      ["request",  "intent:assistance", "⟁Sek"],
  "what":      ["question", "intent:query",      "⟁Yax"],
  "bye":       ["closing",  "intent:farewell",   "⟁Xul"],

  "greeting":  ["hello", "hi", "hey", "good morning"],
  "farewell":  ["bye", "goodbye", "see you", "later"]
}
Composition applies here too. "greeting" is itself a manifest key whose array lists all the surface forms that resolve to it. An ELIZA matcher looks up the input word, gets its array, finds "greeting" in the array, then looks up that key to get the full synonym set — same recursive expansion as CSS and micronauts.

XCFE Inside Manifests

Array values are not limited to strings. A manifest array can contain XCFE control objects — @if/@then/@else — inline. This lets the manifest declare conditional reasoning directly, without a separate logic file. ELIZA / AIML resolves the word, walks the array, and hands XCFE objects to the control-flow engine as it encounters them.

{
  "help": [
    "request",
    "intent:assistance",
    { "@if": "user.authenticated == true",
      "@then": ["⟁Sek", "route:help-center"],
      "@else": ["⟁Wo",  "route:login", "intent:auth-required"] }
  ],

  "buy": [
    "transaction",
    "intent:purchase",
    { "@if": "user.tier >= 1",
      "@then": ["⟁Sek", "route:marketplace"],
      "@else": ["⟁Wo",  "notify:upgrade-required"] }
  ]
}

AIML Templates as Compositional Semantic Programs

A common assumption is that AIML is a chatbot pattern library — pattern → answer string. In this stack, AIML plays a different role: it is a symbolic semantic embedding language. The <template> is not the user-facing reply — it is a structured semantic payload that contributes meaning to the model's context. AIML informs generation; it does not own generation.

Classic AIMLµN-ary role
Output = String Payload = Semantic evidence + State context + Routing signal
pattern → text → user Pattern → F_Q (semantic field) → model context → Model → Reply

A template like "Would you like to teach me a new answer to <get name="badanswer-input"/>?" contains an addressable insertion point — the response is not a fixed string but a structure with a runtime-resolved hole. Combined with <learn> (which generates a new AIML category from runtime data) and <eval> (which resolves dynamically inserted structure), the template becomes a small program that emits text, reads and writes named state, and extends the knowledge base.

Template Complexity — Five Levels Across the ALICE Files

The ALICE corpus shows a clear hierarchy of template complexity. All five levels are present in these files:

LevelModelExample fileDominant operators
0 — Pure projection Answer = String knowledge.aiml none — literal text only
1 — State writing Answer = Text + SET(name) astrology.aiml <set> — answer emits text and writes state simultaneously
2 — Bot property reads Answer = Text + BOT(property) bot.aiml <bot name="..."> — static bot constants (name, location, botmaster, size)
3 — Stochastic + context Answer = RANDOM[...] + GET(x) + PERSON() xfind.aiml <random>, <person/>, <get name="topic">
4 — State machine Answer = StateMachineStep(eindex) personality.aiml <condition name="eindex"> + computed <srai>PERSONALITY TYPE </srai>
5 — Self-modifying Answer = … + EXTEND(Gsemantic) badanswer.aiml <learn> + <eval> — generates new categories from runtime data

Levels 0 and 1 have Answer = String or near-string. Levels 3–5 carry explicit operations over history, context, and bot state. Level 5 is special: <learn> extends Gsemantic at runtime — it adds new vertices (pattern categories) and edges (pattern-template bindings) to the semantic factor of the K'uhul runtime graph. Gphase remains fixed; Gsemantic grows.

personality.aiml introduces a second important pattern: computed srai. The routing target is assembled from state at runtime — <srai>PERSONALITY TYPE <get name="etype"/></srai> routes to a different category depending on the value of etype. This is dynamic dispatch into the pattern space, not routing to a fixed address.

AIML Operators as Address Operators — Complete Table

Every AIML tag is a read, write, transform, or control operation over an indexed state store:

TagClassOperation
<get name="x">READNamed variable x from user state
<set name="x">WRITENamed variable x in user state
<bot name="x">READBot-property constant x (name, location, botmaster, size…)
<input index="n">READUser input history[n]
<that index="n,m">READBot response context[n,m]
<request index="n">READRequest history[n]
<response index="n">READResponse history[n]
<star/>READWildcard capture from current pattern match
<srai>ROUTERoute to another pattern — static or computed from state
<sr/>ROUTEShorthand for <srai><star/></srai> — capture + route
<condition>SELECTBranch on named state value
<random>SELECTStochastic branch from a list
<think>EXECExecute operations without emitting to output
<person/>TRANSFORMPronoun substitution — I↔you, my↔your
<uppercase>TRANSFORMNormalize to uppercase (used in computed srai and learn targets)
<learn>EXTENDAdd a new category to Gsemantic at runtime
<eval>RESOLVEResolve dynamically constructed structure inside <learn>

This is the manifest drive-thru principle at the template layer: symbolic address → resolve → insert/operate. Each tag is an address into an indexed store; the template assembles a response by resolving those addresses at runtime. The address classes (user state, bot constants, history indices, wildcard captures) are the distinct namespaces of the AIML context store.

AIML Categories as SCX Manifest Entries

Because an AIML category carries behavior — not merely text — its natural SCX representation is a full manifest entry. The array encodes the entire semantic structure the category activates:

{
  "⟁BADANSWER": [
    "pattern:BAD ANSWER",
    "state:badanswer-input",
    "state:badanswer-that",
    "state:badanswer-newresp",
    "history:input[2]",
    "history:that[2,1]",
    "topic:badanswer-prelim",
    "route:learning-new-answers",
    "op:condition",
    "op:learn",
    "op:eval"
  ]
}

Now Book(⟁BADANSWER) ≡ N(⟁BADANSWER) contains behavior, not merely words. The manifest neighborhood is: pattern to match, state slots to read and write, history indices, topic context, conditional branch, learning operation, eval operation. This is the horizontal fold unfolding ⟁BADANSWER's book at a single phase:

              ⟁BADANSWER
        ┌─────────┼───────────┐
        │         │           │
     input[2]  that[2,1]    topic
        │         │           │
     history   context    BADANSWER
        │                     │
        ├──── condition ───────┤
        │                     │
        └────── learn ─────────┘
                  │
                 eval

All of these atoms are available at the same phase position — the fold is horizontal. No phase transition is required to inspect the pattern, the state slots, or the routing target. The vertical transition happens only when XCFE authorises execution to cross a phase boundary.

This is what makes AIML an unusually natural source format for SCX atomic Books: the symbolic indirection is already present in the language itself. <get>, <set>, <that>, and <srai> are already address operators over an indexed context store. Mapping them into the manifest namespace requires naming the structure, not inventing new machinery.

A Book in this context contains:

  • Lexical atoms — surface vocabulary
  • AIML patterns — match rules
  • Context references — history indices, topic state
  • XCFE operators — conditional branches, guards
  • State slots — named variables read and written by the template
  • Tracks — fold phase and gravity well affinity
  • Executor references — learn, eval, srai routing

SCX compression keying that full structure is categorically different from keying a word to a shorter label. The symbol is an entry point into a behavioral neighborhood.

AIML = Structural Reasoning · XCFE = Explicit Operator Algebra

The ALICE corpus has no thinking.aiml or reasoning.aiml. This is not an omission — it is a structural property of the language.

AIML reasoning = pattern topology + contextual operators — reasoning emerges from traversing the category graph.
XCFE reasoning = explicit operator algebra — reasoning is named and directly invocable.

They are not competitors. XCFE makes explicit what AIML largely expresses through routing, state, context, and category structure.

AIML Structural Operators — Semantic Role and Graph Interpretation

AIMLSemantic roleGraph interpretation
<pattern>MATCHentry / admission condition — vertex in category graph
<template>EMBEDsemantic payload — model context contribution, not final reply
<condition>SELECTstate-dependent route selection
<srai>ROUTE / CHAINcategory → category arc — forward chaining through Gsemantic
<that>CONTEXTrelationship to previous response state
<input>RECALLinput-history reference
<get>READstate lookup
<set>WRITEstate update
<learn>EXTENDadd structure to Gsemantic
<eval>RESOLVEevaluate dynamic insertion during extension

You get reasoning by chaining <srai> routes through a graph of categories. An srai chain traverses increasingly distant category relationships — A → B → C is effectively A1 → A2 → A3 in adjacency-power terms. A path through the category graph is a reasoning path. There is no <reason> tag because the reasoning is the topology.

PATTERN A
   │
   │ <condition>
   ├──────────────► B
   │
   └──────────────► C
                       │
                       │ <srai>
                       ▼
                   PATTERN D
                       │
                    <that>
                       │
                       ▼
                 context state

<learn> — Runtime Extension of Gsemantic

The <learn> operator is architecturally special. Before learning:

G_runtime,n  =  G_semantic,n  □  G_phase

A <learn> operation produces a new semantic factor:

G_semantic,n+1  =  G_semantic,n  ⊕  ΔG

therefore:

G_runtime,n+1  =  (G_semantic,n ⊕ ΔG)  □  G_phase
                                              ↑
                                     G_phase remains invariant

Runtime learning modifies one factor of the product graph without modifying the control algebra. New categories (vertices) and pattern-template bindings (edges) are added to Gsemantic; the K'uhul phase cycle stays fixed. The learned structure is immediately subject to the same execution laws — it enters the existing product graph, not a separate space.

observe → learn → G_semantic + ΔG → new neighborhoods → new future reasoning paths

XCFE Adds the Missing Explicit Reasoning Verbs

XCFE introduces a named reasoning ISA — operators that make explicit what AIML expresses implicitly through routing:

XCFE operatorImplicit AIML equivalent
XCFE:INFERsrai chain A → B → C
XCFE:DECOMPOSEcondition branch selecting sub-patterns
XCFE:COUNTEREXAMPLEconditional route to contradicting category
XCFE:RECALL<that> / <input index>
XCFE:GENERALIZEwildcard pattern with wide match
XCFE:REVISE<learn> replacing an existing pattern

⟁CAUSE — One SCX Book Spanning Both Systems

Because both AIML structure and XCFE operators live in the manifest array, a single Book spans both without translating either into the other:

{
  "⟁CAUSE": [
    "word:why",
    "word:because",

    "aiml:pattern:WHY *",
    "aiml:srai:CAUSE",
    "aiml:condition:causal",

    "XCFE:DECOMPOSE",
    "XCFE:INFER",
    "XCFE:COUNTEREXAMPLE",

    "track:eliza.intent-reflection",
    "fold:Sek"
  ]
}
Book(⟁CAUSE) ≡ N(⟁CAUSE)
The array is simultaneously the compressed Book and the adjacency declaration. The neighbors don't care that some originated in AIML and others in XCFE — they are semantic neighbors of the same atomic identity.

Consumers don't need the entire Book. An AIML consumer lazily resolves only the pattern and routing pages:

⟁CAUSE  ├─ word:why
        ├─ aiml:pattern:WHY *
        └─ aiml:srai:CAUSE

The XCFE consumer resolves only the operator pages:

⟁CAUSE  ├─ XCFE:DECOMPOSE
        ├─ XCFE:INFER
        └─ XCFE:COUNTEREXAMPLE

Same Book. Same semantic identity. Different requested subsets of the neighborhood. Don't expand the Book; resolve the pages required by the consumer.

Phase Membership ≠ Phase Transition

fold:Sek in the manifest array declares that XCFE:INFER belongs at the Sek phase. It does not execute Sek. This distinction is critical:

Membership(XCFE:INFER, Sek)  ≠  Transition(Yax → Sek)

The manifest can pre-declare the phase membership of every atom in a Book before the graph runs. The runtime at Yax can discover XCFE:INFER as a horizontal neighbor of ⟁CAUSE without leaving Yax:

(⟁CAUSE, Yax)  →horizontal→  (XCFE:INFER, Yax)     Δθ = 0

The operator has been reached but has not executed. XCFE must authorize the vertical step before execution occurs:

(XCFE:INFER, Yax)  →XCFE auth→  (XCFE:INFER, Sek)   Δθ = π/3

Then Sek executes it. Phase membership is declared by the manifest; phase transition is authorized by XCFE. The graph therefore does not have to discover its ontology while running — the manifest has already compressed that ontology into Books; runtime unfolds only the pages it needs.

Complete Execution Pipeline

              SCX KEY
                 │
                 ▼
           MANIFEST BOOK
                 │
          N(key) adjacency
                 │
      ┌──────────┴──────────┐
      │                     │
 AIML STRUCTURE        XCFE OPERATORS
 pattern/context       explicit reasoning
 condition/srai        executor routes
 learn/state           control contracts
      │                     │
      └──────────┬──────────┘
                 │
           HORIZONTAL FOLD
               Δθ = 0
                 │
               YAX
          semantic field F_Q
                 │
           XCFE AUTHORITY
                 │
           VERTICAL FOLD
              Δθ = π/3
                 │
               SEK
         semantic evidence
                 │
               MODEL
         response generation
                 │
               REPLY
             (to user)
AIML informs generation; it does not own generation.
AIML and XCFE supply semantic field evidence to the model. The model synthesises the reply. SCX pre-declares the ontology into Books so the runtime only unfolds the pages it needs — it never has to discover structure while running.

AIML as Symbolic Semantic Embedding

AIML's constructs are reinterpreted in this stack as semantic features feeding the model, not as response generators. The original machinery is preserved; its role changes:

AIML constructµN-ary role
<pattern>Lexical/semantic activation feature — identifies seed atoms in the query
<topic>Field/domain embedding — scopes the semantic region being activated
<that>Contextual relationship feature — prior-response state as adjacency signal
<condition>Conditional semantic feature — state-dependent field routing
<srai>Association/routing edge — graph arc between semantic nodes
<template>Semantic payload / model context — retrievable evidence, not final reply
<get>/<set>State features — slot reads/writes as semantic context signals
<learn>Persistent semantic-topology extension — ΔZ, changes future field activation

The template text ("There may be several reasons for <star/>.") is valuable because it supplies meaning about the pattern — not because it is printed to the user. It encodes the association Pattern ↔ ResponseMeaning, which becomes retrievable semantic evidence about what WHY * means and what kind of information belongs around it.

AIML + N-Gram: Two Complementary Embedding Systems

µN-ary RAG now has two symbolic embedding systems, neither of which requires dense vectors:

AIML embedding     — structured meaning: pattern, topic, context, template payload
N-gram embedding   — association evidence: co-occurrence, frequency, n-ary combinations

E_semantic  =  E_AIML  +  E_n-gram   (complementary, not interchangeable)

They answer different questions about the same input:

LayerPrimary question
AIMLWhat kind of semantic pattern is this?
N-gramsWhat symbols/terms tend to occur together?
SCX BookWhat neighborhood belongs to this identity?
Graph κHow internally coherent is that neighborhood?
F_QWhat part of the topology is relevant right now?

For WHY DID THE CAR STOP, the two systems converge independently:

AIML:    WHY *  →  CAUSAL_QUESTION  →  ⟁CAUSE
N-grams: (CAR, STOP), (WHY, CAR, STOP)  →  cause/failure cluster

        AIML: WHY *
              │
              ▼
           ⟁CAUSE
          /       \
ngram:WHY-CAR   ngram:CAR-STOP
          \       /
            F_Q

If AIML identifies a causal pattern, the grams strongly associate the input with cause/failure, and the graph around ⟁CAUSE has high κ, Yax receives several independent structural signals pointing at the same semantic region. Their agreement is evidence; their disagreement is also informative — AIML supplies authored structural semantics, grams supply observed association. The semantic field is where evidence from both meets.

A third channel — conventional dense vector embedding — remains an optional evidence contributor on the same path. None of the three channels holds execution authority.

Two Channels — Form and Content

Micronauts belong inside the n-ary channel, not as a third embedding. The query fans out into two parallel channels that converge on F_Q:

                  USER QUERY
                      │
          ┌───────────┴───────────┐
          ▼                       ▼
   ① AIML EMBEDDING        ② N-ARY + µ EMBEDDING
   pattern structure         grams + Micronauts
          │                       │
   recognizes FORM          recognizes CONTENT
   ⟁CONDITIONAL             organic-compounds
   ⟁EVIDENCE                planetary-atmosphere
   ⟁IMPLICATION             compounds→life
   ⟁CAUSE                   biosignature
   ⟁CERTAINTY               SCIENCE-µ / REASON-µ / VERIFY-µ
          │                       │
          └───────────┬───────────┘
                      ▼
                SEMANTIC FIELD F_Q
                      │
                     YAX
               admission / attention
                      │
                     XCFE
                      │
                     SEK
               semantic evidence
                      │
                  P(F_Q) → LLM → Reply

For "If organic compounds are visible in a planet's atmosphere, doesn't that mean the planet has life on it?" the two channels answer different questions independently:

ChannelWhat it recognizesContribution to F_Q
① AIML structural IF X — DOESN'T THAT MEAN Y? — evidence-entailment question ⟁CONDITIONAL, ⟁EVIDENCE, ⟁IMPLICATION, ⟁CAUSE, ⟁CERTAINTY
② N-ary + µ Planetary atmospheric chemistry as evidence for life grams: (ORGANIC, ATMOSPHERE, LIFE, BIOSIGNATURE); specialists: SCIENCE-µ, REASON-µ, VERIFY-µ

Channel ① says: "This query asks whether evidence X entails conclusion Y." Channel ② says: "The question concerns biosignature chemistry." Neither channel answers the astronomy question. Together they construct a richer problem representation for the model.

Micronaut routing belongs in channel ② because Micronauts are activated by semantic content, not syntactic form. The gram score R_µ(Q) = cos(Q, K_µ) surfaces the appropriate specialists; Yax decides via S_µ = W_µ · C_µ · R_µ(Q) which enter the field.

The admitted field projected to the LLM approximates:

QUERY STRUCTURE:   evidence(X) → implies?(Y)
X:                 organic compounds, planetary atmosphere
Y:                 biological life
CONCEPTS:          organic chemistry, atmospheric chemistry, biosignature,
                   abiotic process, evidence, implication, certainty
REASONING:         INFER, COMPARE, COUNTEREXAMPLE, VERIFY
SPECIALISTS:       SCIENCE-µ, REASON-µ, VERIFY-µ

The LLM synthesizes the actual explanation — distinguishing "organic molecules are evidence worth investigating" from "organic molecules logically prove life" — without AIML ever composing the reply.

Same Form, Different Subject, Different Capability

Because the two channels operate on different aspects of the query, the same AIML structural embedding can route completely different Micronauts depending on what the n-ary channel sees.

Both queries below share essentially the same Channel ① structure:

IF X → DOESN'T THAT MEAN Y?   →   E_A: { CONDITIONAL, EVIDENCE, IMPLICATION, CERTAINTY }

But Channel ② produces different semantic fields:

Query A: "If organic compounds are in a planet's atmosphere, doesn't that mean it has life?"
  E_N: { ORGANIC, ATMOSPHERE, LIFE, BIOSIGNATURE }
       → SCIENCE-µ, REASON-µ, VERIFY-µ

Query B: "If this function returns null, doesn't that mean the object wasn't created?"
  E_N: { FUNCTION, NULL, RETURN, OBJECT, CREATION }
       → CODE-µ, REASON-µ, VERIFY-µ

SCIENCE-µ dominates the first; CODE-µ dominates the second. Neither Micronaut is encoded into AIML patterns — the grams expose relevant capabilities, R_µ(Q) measures alignment, W_µ · C_µ supplies learned competence, and Yax admits only the ones relevant to the current F_Q. This confirms that Micronauts belong on Channel ②: they are admitted because of semantic content, not because AIML pattern-matched their name.

Tripartite Abstraction

The two channels plus Micronaut admission establish a clean three-level division of responsibility:

LayerDeterminesExample
AIML — Channel ①Reasoning shapeEvidence-entailment question → ⟁CONDITIONAL, ⟁IMPLICATION
N-ary — Channel ②Reasoning subjectPlanetary chemistry → ORGANIC, ATMOSPHERE, BIOSIGNATURE
Micronauts — admitted via ②Reasoning capabilitySCIENCE-µ, REASON-µ, VERIFY-µ enter F_Q

The full field combines all three contributions:

F_Q  =  E_A(Q)  ⊕  E_N(Q)  ⊕  µ_admitted(Q)

No single layer answers the question. AIML supplies the logical structure; n-ary grams supply the domain content; admitted Micronauts supply the specialist capabilities to work on it. The model receives the combined field and synthesizes the reply.

FORM + MEANING → CAPABILITY → REASONING → EXPRESSION
AIML  +  N-ARY  →  MICRONAUT  →  XCFE  →  LLM

F_Q = E_A(Q) ⊕ E_N(Q) ⊕ μadmitted(Q)

E_A(Q) — structural embedding: what is the reasoning shape?
E_N(Q) — content embedding: what is the reasoning subject?
μadmitted — capability: which specialists are relevant to that content?

AIML, n-ary grams, and Micronauts are not competing retrieval mechanisms — they are sequential layers of a problem-representation pipeline.

Graph Query — Semantic Interrogation Layer

Semantic activation and semantic interrogation are distinct operations. Producing F_Q answers "which nodes are relevant?" — it is a subset. Querying F_Q answers "what topology actually connects them?" — it is a structural read. The graph query layer sits between the activated field and Yax admission:

Q  →  (E_A ⊕ E_N+µ)  →  F_Q  →  Query(F_Q, pattern)  →  Evidence  →  Yax  →  XCFE  →  Model  →  Reply

Note that the query is scoped to F_Q, not to all of Gsemantic. FQ is the scope; the query is the read within that scope. Query cost is bounded by context size, not by full graph size.

Three Layers, Three Questions

The first two embedding channels produce the activated field. The graph query layer interrogates it. XCFE acts on the interrogation result.

#LayerQuestion answered
①AIML — Channel ①What kind of question is being asked? (form)
②N-ary + µ — Channel ②What is it about and who should work on it? (subject + capability)
③Graph queryWhat relationships actually exist between these things? (structural evidence)
④XCFEWhat do those connections authorize? (reasoning authority)

① and ② both produce activation (FQ). ③ interrogates the activated field. ④ acts on the interrogation result. Each layer answers a question the previous layer cannot.

Cypher-Like Graph Pattern Syntax

A declarative property-graph query language (Cypher is the canonical example) maps cleanly onto Gsemantic. A match describes topology sought; the engine returns matching subgraphs:

-- path query: what declared paths connect ORGANIC to LIFE?
MATCH (organic)-[path*]->(life)
WHERE organic IN F_Q AND life IN F_Q
RETURN path

-- arc property query: which relationships carry a specific arc type?
MATCH (a)-[r:POSSIBLE_BIOSIGNATURE]->(b)
RETURN a, r, b

This maps onto ARC semantics directly. Cypher relationships are directed, named, and carry properties — the same structure as ARC(X, Z, W, T, ΔH):

(ORGANIC)-[:POSSIBLE_BIOSIGNATURE {weight:0.7, phase:Sek}]->(LIFE)

corresponds to:
ARC( ORGANIC, LIFE, w=0.7, type=biosignature, ΔH=phase-offset )

Embedding similarity produces a flat set of relevant nodes. The graph query returns the declared semantic paths connecting them — the system is no longer relying exclusively on proximity in embedding space.

Graph Query Is a Deterministic Executor

The graph query layer is not a fourth embedding. It is a deterministic executor in the same category as XCFE — structural, reproducible, O(graph-traversal). The executor family:

ExecutorOperation
graph.queryCypher-like pattern match within FQ
graph.pathReachability / shortest path between two nodes
graph.neighborBook(v) expansion — N(v) at depth k
graph.arcARC(X, Z, W, T, ΔH) property read on a named relationship
graph.patternSubgraph isomorphism — does FQ contain this shape?

Updated Stack

LayerFunction
AIMLStructural embedding — reasoning shape
N-ary gramsContent embedding — reasoning subject
MicronautsCapability routing — admitted via Channel ②
SCX BooksSymbolic neighborhoods — Book(v) ≡ N(v)
FQActive semantic field — context-activated region of Gsemantic
Graph queryStructural interrogation — Query(FQ, pattern)
YaxAdmission / attention — what evidence enters the model context
XCFEReasoning authority — what the evidence permits
SekExecution — acts authorized by XCFE
LLMLinguistic synthesis — Reply
Four-Pattern Symmetry

AIML pattern → semantic field → graph pattern → reasoning pattern

AIML tells you where to look. N-ary tells you what matters there.
Graph query tells you what is actually connected there.
XCFE decides what those connections permit you to infer or do.

XCFE Pipeline Rules — Phase-Gated Execution

XCFE rules are declared as JSONL objects. Each rule carries a condition, an action, and a priority. Rules that fire on phase state must explicitly gate on the phase field — a derived-field condition alone is not a phase guard.

Rule Format

{"type": "xcfe_rule", "rule_id": "P-001", "data": {
  "name":      "rule_name",
  "condition": {"field": "...", "operator": "==", "value": "..."},
  "action":    {"type": "dispatch|parallel", "target": "...", "params": {}},
  "priority":  10
}}

Compound conditions use {"op": "and", "conditions": [...]}. A rule that conditions only on a derived field (e.g. route_decision, verify_result.score) without a phase guard can fire at any phase if the field is set.

Six-Phase Pipeline Mapping

RulePhase gateStageAdditional condition
P-001Popingest—
P-002Worouter—
P-003Yaxbranch (parallel)route_decision == complex
P-004Sekcompute—
P-005Ch'enverify—
P-006Xulemitscore ≥ 0.85
P-007Xulretry → routescore < 0.85, max 3

Three Correctness Rules

RuleViolationFix
Phase names must be exact "Chen" never matches — the phase is "Ch'en" Use the apostrophe. Six names: Pop Wo Yax Sek Ch'en Xul
Phase-stage rules must carry a phase gate Condition on derived field alone fires at any phase Compound: phase == X AND derived_field == value
Retry loops must declare a forward-progress bound Unbounded retry = unclosed manifold = compile error in cs_5_0 Add max_retries and halt_on_convergence: true

Corrected Pipeline JSONL

// Generated from pipeline_example.@.pipeline
// Phase → Stage → Rule mapping

{"type":"xcfe_rule","rule_id":"P-001","data":{"name":"ingest_pop","condition":{"field":"phase","operator":"==","value":"Pop"},"action":{"type":"dispatch","target":"ingest","params":{"tensor":"512x512"}},"priority":10}}

{"type":"xcfe_rule","rule_id":"P-002","data":{"name":"route_wo","condition":{"field":"phase","operator":"==","value":"Wo"},"action":{"type":"dispatch","target":"router","params":{"query":"classify"}},"priority":9}}

{"type":"xcfe_rule","rule_id":"P-003","data":{"name":"branch_yax","condition":{"op":"and","conditions":[{"field":"phase","operator":"==","value":"Yax"},{"field":"route_decision","operator":"==","value":"complex"}]},"action":{"type":"parallel","target":"branch","params":{"nodes":2}},"priority":8}}

{"type":"xcfe_rule","rule_id":"P-004","data":{"name":"compute_sek","condition":{"field":"phase","operator":"==","value":"Sek"},"action":{"type":"dispatch","target":"compute","params":{"layers":"256,128,64,10"}},"priority":7}}

{"type":"xcfe_rule","rule_id":"P-005","data":{"name":"verify_chen","condition":{"field":"phase","operator":"==","value":"Ch'en"},"action":{"type":"dispatch","target":"verify","params":{"threshold":0.85}},"priority":6}}

{"type":"xcfe_rule","rule_id":"P-006","data":{"name":"emit_xul","condition":{"op":"and","conditions":[{"field":"phase","operator":"==","value":"Xul"},{"field":"verify_result.score","operator":">=","value":0.85}]},"action":{"type":"dispatch","target":"emit","params":{"priority":"high"}},"priority":5}}

{"type":"xcfe_rule","rule_id":"P-007","data":{"name":"retry_loop","condition":{"op":"and","conditions":[{"field":"phase","operator":"==","value":"Xul"},{"field":"verify_result.score","operator":"<","value":0.85}]},"action":{"type":"dispatch","target":"route","params":{"max_retries":3,"halt_on_convergence":true}},"priority":4}}
Priority ordering decreases with phase order (10 → 4). This is a convention, not a requirement, but it makes evaluation order match the phase sequence. P-007's retry dispatches back to route — a bounded loop. Without max_retries the loop has no provable exit and the grammar rejects it.

Custom SCX Patterns — Authoring the Semantic Graph

The manifest is an open key→array registry. You define any key, any array, any namespace prefix for array values. The only fixed constraints are: @-prefix is reserved for runtime operators; phase names must be actual C₆ phases; array value namespaces need to be recognized by the consuming runtime. Everything else is yours to declare.

{
  "⟁GREET": [
    "word:hello",
    "word:hi",
    "myapp:intent:greeting",
    "myapp:pattern:HELLO *",
    "XCFE:RECALL",
    "track:salutation",
    "fold:Pop"
  ],

  "⟁NEGOTIATE": [
    "myapp:pattern:HOW MUCH *",
    "myapp:pattern:WHAT IS THE PRICE *",
    "XCFE:COMPARE",
    "XCFE:INFER",
    "state:offer-floor",
    "state:offer-ceiling",
    "fold:Sek"
  ]
}

Adding a manifest entry is extending Gsemantic. New keys are new vertices. Array values are new edges. You are not configuring a system — you are authoring a semantic graph that the runtime mounts.

SCX Manifest as Semantic Association Store

The classical Lisp semantic network uses association lists:

(canary
  (is-a bird)
  (color yellow)
  (size small))

This is N(canary) = { (is-a, bird), (color, yellow), (size, small) } — a neighborhood declaration. The SCX manifest expresses the same structure in its own symbolic form:

{
  "⟁CANARY": [
    "is-a:⟁BIRD",
    "color:⟁YELLOW",
    "size:⟁SMALL"
  ],

  "⟁PENGUIN": [
    "is-a:⟁BIRD",
    "movement:⟁SWIM"
  ],

  "⟁BIRD": [
    "is-a:⟁VERTEBRATE",
    "has-part:⟁WINGS",
    "reproduction:⟁EGG-LAYING"
  ]
}

The manifest is an atomic semantic association store. The associations chain through recursive key resolution — no duplication required at any level:

⟁CANARY
├── is-a ──► ⟁BIRD
│             ├── is-a ──────► ⟁VERTEBRATE
│             ├── has-part ──► ⟁WINGS
│             └── reproduction ► ⟁EGG-LAYING
├── color ─► ⟁YELLOW
└── size ──► ⟁SMALL

Book expansion is recursive:

Book(⟁CANARY) → Book(⟁BIRD) → Book(⟁VERTEBRATE)

The facts about ⟁BIRD are declared once. ⟁CANARY, ⟁PENGUIN, and any other bird-subtype inherit them through resolution, not duplication. That is simultaneously the association mechanism and the compression mechanism — they are the same operation.

Word Association Without Embedding Models

The key does not need to represent a formal ontology. Word associations are equally first-class:

{
  "⟁WHY": [
    "assoc:⟁CAUSE",
    "assoc:⟁REASON",
    "assoc:⟁EXPLANATION",
    "assoc:⟁BECAUSE"
  ],

  "⟁CAUSE": [
    "word:why",
    "word:because",
    "word:therefore",
    "concept:causality",
    "XCFE:INFER",
    "XCFE:COUNTEREXAMPLE",
    "fold:Sek"
  ]
}

Encountering the word why resolves to ⟁WHY, which resolves to ⟁CAUSE, which horizontally unfolds to {because, therefore, causality, XCFE:INFER, XCFE:COUNTEREXAMPLE}. That is genuine symbolic word association — the relationship is declared in the manifest and resolved at O(1) per hop, without asking an embedding model to rediscover it on every inference pass.

AIML + SCX + Phases — The Full Pipeline

When AIML matches WHY *, the match is the lexical admission gate. The semantic work happens in the SCX Book:

WHY * (AIML pattern match — lexical admission)
  ↓
⟁WHY → ⟁CAUSE
  ↓
Book(⟁CAUSE) = { word:because, word:therefore, concept:causality,
                 XCFE:INFER, XCFE:COUNTEREXAMPLE, fold:Sek }
  ↓
Yax — which associations matter for this query?  (Δθ = 0)
  ↓
XCFE — is XCFE:INFER authorized?
  ↓
Sek — execute it  (Δθ = π/3)

AIML performs the lexical / pattern admission. SCX supplies the associations. Yax selects which associations are relevant. XCFE controls whether an operator is admitted. Sek executes it. Four distinct responsibilities, one shared substrate.

Three Levels of Association — Atomic, Binary, N-ary

A Lisp association list is centered around key → [(relation, value), …]. The manifest can do that, but the n-ary gram system adds a third level: the combination itself carries semantic significance.

LevelFormMeaning
Atomic ⟁WHY Stable symbolic identity — entry point into a Book
Binary association ⟁WHY ── assoc ──► ⟁CAUSE Named directed relationship between two identities
N-ary gram g(⟁WHY, ⟁CAUSE, ⟁BECAUSE, XCFE:INFER) The co-occurrence itself carries meaning — not reducible to any single binary edge

All three levels are horizontally traversable at the same θ — no phase transition required to inspect any of them.

The Fundamental Operation

KEY → ARRAY  →  KEY → NEIGHBORHOOD  →  Book(k) ≡ N(k)

k → N(k) → N(N(k)) → N(N(N(k))) → …

Recursive key resolution gives you the full semantic network. The associations are the compression — not something added on top of it.

SCX does not need a separate semantic-network format. The manifest is the graph. Each entry extends it. The semantic network isn't added on top of compression — the associations are the compression.

SCX Manifest = symbol dictionary + semantic topology + XCFE traversal contract
The manifest does not only compress what a symbol means. It can compress how the structure may be traversed.

BFS and DFS — Two Modes of Horizontal Unfold

Both BFS and DFS traverse semantic topology without advancing the K'uhul phase angle. Both are horizontal operations — Δθ = 0. They differ in the order of neighborhood expansion.

BFS = Breadth Horizontal Unfold

BFS is almost literally the horizontal-fold operation. Starting from X, it explores the current neighborhood before going deeper:

DEPTH 0             X

DEPTH 1       A     B     C

DEPTH 2     D E     F     G H

Mathematically: N₀[X] → N₁[X] → N₂[X] → … This gives a deterministic implementation for:

HF_k(X) = N_k[X]     — the depth-k horizontal fold of X

BFS finding a candidate does not mean it executes. It becomes a Yax candidate:

BFS → Yax → XCFE → Sek

A semantic query such as "find the nearest Micronaut capable of MathML evaluation" naturally uses BFS — explore nearby semantic nodes, associated grams, tracks, and micronauts; stop at the first valid candidate. Admission is Yax; execution is Sek.

DFS = Depth Horizontal Unfold

DFS follows one relational branch deeply before returning:

X → A → D → H → …    (discovery)
              H → D → A → X    (fold back)

This maps to recursive semantic investigation — following a causal chain, an inheritance hierarchy, or a reasoning path to its end before backtracking. DFS also produces a structural distinction useful for the Brain:

Edge typeMeaningCh'en use
Discovery arcX → Z, Z not yet seenUnfold a new candidate
Back arcX → Z, Z already knownDetect cycle, dependency, or already-resolved structure

Ch'en can inspect the traversal results — discovery arcs expose new semantic content; back arcs signal recursion, known relations, or convergence points.

XCFE Inside the Manifest — Compressing Traversal Contracts

Because manifest keys can reference other manifest keys, a traversal algorithm is itself compressible. Instead of writing the BFS queue algorithm inline every time, the manifest can declare a canonical traversal key:

{
  "⟁WHY": {
    "atoms":    ["word:why", "word:cause", "word:because"],
    "graph":    { "root": "concept:cause", "traversal": "⟁BFS", "depth": 3 },
    "xcfe":     ["XCFE:INFER", "XCFE:COUNTEREXAMPLE", "XCFE:VERIFY"]
  },

  "⟁BFS": {
    "algorithm": "breadth-first",
    "scope":     "neighborhood",
    "admission": "Yax",
    "execution": "Sek"
  },

  "⟁INFER": [
    "@phase:Sek",
    "executor:semantic.query",
    "output:observation"
  ]
}

⟁WHY now compresses an entire semantic workflow — lexical atoms, semantic root, graph traversal algorithm, depth constraint, reasoning operators, admission phase, execution phase — into one atomic key. ⟁BFS is a manifest key like any other; its definition establishes the traversal semantics within this manifest's authority scope.

The key is not magical. It works because:

Key  →  manifest resolution  →  CanonicalDefinition  →  XCFE  →  AuthorizedMeaning

SCX is not saying ⟁BFS somehow inherently means breadth-first search. It is saying: within this manifest authority, ⟁BFS canonically resolves to this known operational definition. That gives deterministic decompression.

Other traversal algorithms follow the same pattern:

⟁BFS  · ⟁DFS  · ⟁NEIGHBOR  · ⟁PATH  · ⟁SHORTEST  · ⟁CYCLE  · ⟁TOPO

Recursive Symbolic Compression

A key can contain keys whose definitions contain other keys. The symbolic chain compounds:

⟁WHY → ⟁CAUSE → ⟁BFS → N_k[X]

K₀ → [K₁, K₂, K₃]
K₁ → [K₄, K₅]
K₄ → […]

A tiny surface representation addresses a very large semantic structure. The manifest array is simultaneously the compression encoding and the adjacency list:

Compression view:  ⟁WHY ⟹ compressed composition
Graph view:        N(⟁WHY) = {⟁CAUSE, ⟁INFER, ⟁BFS, ⟁VERIFY}

BFS and DFS are therefore not merely algorithms the system happens to include. They become the two fundamental modes of decompressing the symbolic neighborhood:

ModeOperationUse
SCX key →BFSBreadth horizontal unfoldNearest-candidate queries
SCX key →DFSDepth horizontal unfoldRecursive chain investigation

XCFE determines whether and how far that unfolding is legally allowed to proceed. The manifest declares the neighborhood and the traversal contract; XCFE authorizes traversal across phase boundaries; the K'uhul phase cycle remains invariant throughout.

Full Pipeline — All Layers in Order

AIML pattern recognizes WHY *       — lexical admission
          ↓
SCX: WHY → ⟁WHY → opens Book       — symbolic resolution
          ↓
Horizontal Fold: traverse N(⟁WHY), N²(⟁WHY)…   Δθ = 0
          ↓
Yax: select relevant associations / operators
          ↓
XCFE: authorize proposed operation
          ↓
Sek: execute                         Δθ = π/3
          ↓
Ch'en: observe / verify (back edges, discovery edges)
          ↓
Xul: collapse to compact symbolic state

The model does not manufacture the association between why, causality, and inference on every pass. The manifest already encoded it.

Attention, Context, and the Manifest

One refinement sharpens the picture: attention is contextual selection, not context itself.

The manifest gives the available context:

N(k) = declared contextual neighborhood of k

Attention is the operation that determines which parts of that context matter now:

Attention(Q, k) = Select(Q, N_k[k])

The pieces separate cleanly across the stack:

LayerFunction
SCX manifestDeclares possible semantic context
N(k)Immediate contextual field
BFSBroadens context by neighborhood depth
DFSFollows a contextual chain deeply
N-ary gramsExpose higher-order contextual configurations
YaxSelects / ranks currently relevant context — symbolic attention
XCFEAuthorizes what selected context may cause
SekPerforms the operation
Ch'enObserves / verifies what happened
XulCollapses the contextual result
Manifest = sparse symbolic context / association matrix
Yax traversal + selection = symbolic attention

The Manifest as Sparse Adjacency Matrix

Given keys K = {k₁, k₂, …, kₙ}, the manifest induces a sparse adjacency matrix:

M_ij = 1   if k_j ∈ N(k_i)
M_ij = 0   otherwise

— or weighted:  M_ij = w(k_i, k_j)

The manifest entry:

{ "⟁WHY": ["⟁CAUSE", "⟁INFER", "⟁COUNTEREXAMPLE"] }

is one sparse row of that matrix:

           WHY   CAUSE   INFER   COUNTEREXAMPLE   …
WHY         0      1       1           1

SCX does not store the zeros. The array records only the nonzero semantic associations — that itself is compression. BFS then computes a broader attention field N_k[WHY]; DFS produces an attention path WHY → CAUSE → EXPLANATION → …; Yax determines what is admitted from that field.

Transformer Comparison

TransformerSCX / K'uhul
Context All positions; dense learned weights Declared sparse neighborhood N(k)
Selection softmax(QKᵀ / √d_k) BFS/DFS → Yax filter
Relationship source Recovered dynamically from learned representations Declared symbolically; traversal exposes; Yax selects
Phase boundary None — attention is undifferentiated across dimensions XCFE authorization required to cross θ boundary

The two can coexist. A model can provide a residual relevance term Rmodel(Q, k_i) without owning the topology:

S_i = W_i × C_i × R_i(Q)

  W_i  — manifest-declared association weight
  C_i  — declared context entry (neighbor in N(k))
  R_i  — model's residual relevance score

  S_i operates inside the manifest-derived candidate field
  rather than asking dense attention to rediscover the entire
  semantic relationship structure from scratch.

Two Axes — Context vs Control

One correction to any temptation to call Δθ = π/3 "vertical attention": it is not attention — it is a phase transition. The two axes of the product graph carry distinct responsibilities:

AxisRole
Horizontal (Δθ = 0)Context / attention / traversal
Vertical (Δθ = π/3)Control / phase transition
 
                    HORIZONTAL
             CONTEXT / ATTENTION / TRAVERSAL

         A ←─── X ───→ B
         │      │       │
         C      D       E
                │
    BFS / DFS / Yax selection
────────────────────────────────────
              θ = 2π/3  YAX
────────────────────────────────────
                │
                │  XCFE authorization
                ▼
              θ = π  SEK

                 VERTICAL
             PHASE / CONTROL

Architectural Law and Final Definition

SCX declares context
      → Traversal exposes context
      → Yax attends
      → XCFE authorizes
      → Sek acts
SCX compresses context by storing semantic adjacency rather than repeatedly expanding semantic context.

Because Book(k) ≡ N(k), the same atomic manifest structure simultaneously serves as compression dictionary, semantic neighborhood, and context substrate. That is the convergence.

Book Coherence — Clustering Coefficient in Gsemantic

Book size and Book coherence are not the same thing. |N(v)| counts how many nodes are in a Book; the clustering coefficient measures how strongly those nodes are associated with one another, independent of their shared connection to the Book's key.

For a node v, the local clustering coefficient is:

κ(v) = 2E_v / (k_v(k_v − 1))

where:
  k_v = |N(v)|    — Book size (number of neighbors)
  E_v             — edges that exist among those neighbors

κ(v) = 1   →  all neighbors connect to each other  (maximal coherence)
κ(v) = 0   →  no neighbors connect                 (pure star — all spokes, no rim)
MeasureSymbolWhat it counts
Book size|N(v)|How many atoms are in the neighborhood
Book coherenceκ(v)How strongly those atoms associate with each other
Network coherenceκ(Gs)Average local clustering across the full semantic graph

A Book can be large and semantically sloppy, or compact and densely closed:

|N(v)| = 100,  κ(v) ≈ 0   →  large star; many atoms, few cross-links between them
|N(v)| =   6,  κ(v) ≈ 1   →  tight cluster; every atom confirms every other

The second is a stronger semantic unit even though it is far smaller.

Triangle Semantics in SCX

The triangle formulation makes the structural meaning explicit:

κ(v) = λ_G(v) / τ_G(v)

  λ_G(v)  — triangles containing v
  τ_G(v)  — triples (connected paths of length 2) centered on v

Adjacency form:  A_ij · A_jk · A_ki  detects each closed three-step loop

A closed triangle has a concrete SCX meaning. For ⟁CAUSE with neighbors WHY and REASON:

Open (star):      WHY ← ⟁CAUSE → REASON
                  WHY and REASON do not confirm each other independently.

Closed (triangle): WHY ← ⟁CAUSE → REASON
                                └──────────▶ WHY
                  WHY↔REASON exists independently of ⟁CAUSE.
                  The association survives even if ⟁CAUSE is removed.

The closed triangle is structurally stronger because the Book has internal cohesion, not just hub connectivity. The n-ary gram WHY·REASON·CAUSE records this closure as evidence; the clustering coefficient measures how many such closures exist across the entire neighborhood.

Clustering as Structural Evidence in Ranking

Clustering enters the ranking formula as structural evidence — not authority. Using κ to avoid collision with Ciearned:

R_i(Q)  =  f(query relevance,  gram evidence,  path evidence,  κ_i)

S_i  =  W_i · C_i^earned · R_i(Q, κ_i)

A candidate with high κ is a tighter semantic cluster — its Book members confirm one another. Yax uses that as one structural signal alongside gram frequency and path length. Coherence is evidence, not a gate.

Deterministic — Derived from Gsemantic Itself

Clustering coefficient requires no language model. Ch'en can calculate κ(v) for any node deterministically from the manifest-declared adjacency graph:

ATOM               ⟁CAUSE
BOOK               N(⟁CAUSE)
BOOK SIZE          |N(⟁CAUSE)|
BOOK COHERENCE     κ(⟁CAUSE)
NETWORK COHERENCE  κ(G_semantic)

Word association in SCX therefore has a verifiable structural dimension. It is not enough to declare that WHY → CAUSE; the graph can measure whether WHY↔REASON, REASON↔BECAUSE, BECAUSE↔AIML_causal, and AIML_causal↔XCFE:INFER all close as triangles. That verification is fully deterministic, derived from the graph itself, and independent of any model output.

Book size ≠ Book coherence
|N(v)| counts atoms — κ(v) = 2E_v / k_v(k_v−1) measures how strongly they confirm each other. A compact tightly-closed Book outperforms a large sparse one as a semantic evidence unit. N-ary grams provide higher-order association evidence; clustering coefficient gives a deterministic graph-derived measure of neighborhood closure. Both are calculated from Gsemantic — no model inference required.

Semantic Fields — Context-Activated Regions of Gsemantic

A semantic field is not another synonym for the graph or for a fold. It is a precise concept: a bounded, temporary subset of Gsemantic that a specific context activates. The user context does not equal the field — it induces it.

F_Q  =  { v ∈ G_semantic | v is activated/relevant under context Q }

A query like "Why does a canary have wings?" activates a bounded region of the full topology:

F_Q  =  { CANARY, BIRD, WINGS, ANIMAL, has-part, WHY, CAUSE }

G_semantic contains all known semantic topology — animals, physics, mathematics, ...
F_Q is the context-relevant slice. The rest of G_semantic is not activated.

Field Induction Path

The runtime constructs F_Q by detecting seed atoms from the query and unfolding their Books:

Q  →  Seeds(Q)  =  { ⟁CANARY, ⟁WINGS, ⟁CAUSE }

F_Q  =  ⋃_{v ∈ Seeds(Q)}  Unfold_k(Book(v))
        subject to relevance and admission constraints (Yax)

User Context → Seed Atoms → Books → Horizontal Unfold → Semantic Field

Each component has a defined role in building F_Q:

ComponentRole in field construction
AIMLIdentifies and routes the initial seed atoms from the query pattern
N-ary gramsIdentify higher-order atom combinations active within F_Q
Book(v) = N(v)Expands each seed into its neighborhood — the unfold unit that populates F_Q
κ(v)Measures local coherence of each Book contributing to F_Q
Yax attentionDetermines which portions of F_Q are relevant enough to admit into active context

Fields Are Temporary Subsets

Gsemantic is permanent — the full declared topology. F_Q is a temporary slice bounded by the current context. Different queries activate different fields; fields can overlap where their seed atoms share neighborhoods:

F_Q  ⊆  G_semantic          — canary query activates a region
F_Q' ⊆  G_semantic          — physics query activates a different region
F_Q ∩ F_Q' ≠ ∅              — if they share atoms (e.g., both activate CAUSE)

Context is preserved without duplicating the underlying knowledge. Two conversations share the same Gsemantic and instantiate separate fields over it.

Sharpened Runtime State

The product-graph runtime position P = (v, k, θ) sharpens to a field-state pair when the active context region is made explicit:

State_t  =  (F_t, θ_t)

  F_t ⊆ G_semantic   — active semantic field at time t
  θ_t ∈ G_phase      — current K'uhul phase (C₆)

The two fold types operate on different coordinates of this state:

Horizontal fold:   (F_t, θ) → (F_{t+1}, θ)     Δθ = 0
                   Expands, refines, or narrows the active field.
                   Kernel(T_φ) operates within F_t at fixed phase.

Vertical fold:     (F, θ)   → (F, θ + π/3)      ΔF = 0
                   Advances the phase. The active field persists unchanged.
                   XCFE authorization required to cross the phase boundary.
Precise vocabulary — graph vs field vs book
Semantic graph G_semantic = all known semantic topology — permanent, declared by manifests
Semantic field F_Q = context-activated region of that topology — temporary, query-bounded
Book(v) = N(v) = one atom's addressable neighborhood — the unfold unit that builds fields

Context activates Fields. Books unfold Fields. Horizontal Folds traverse Fields. Vertical Folds advance the phase over Fields.

The Semantic Hierarchy

The section ordering establishes a four-level containment hierarchy, each level more specific and less persistent than the one above:

G_semantic         — persistent topology  (all declared semantic knowledge)
  ⊇
F_Q                — context-activated region  (temporary, query-bounded)
  ⊇
N(v) = Book(v)     — one atom's addressable neighborhood  (unfold unit)
  ⊇
v                  — atomic semantic identity  (manifest key)

v is atomic and permanent. Book(v) is the neighborhood declared around it. F_Q is the bounded slice activated by context — temporary, built from Book unfolds. G_semantic is the total declared space; it is never fully activated at once.

Three Axes — 3D K'uhul State Evolution

Learning is not a third operation that sits alongside horizontal and vertical folds as a peer. It is the Z-axis produced by their interaction: the accumulated structural depth that each 2π cycle can leave behind.

X — Horizontal Fold   — semantic association / field traversal   (ΔF ≠ 0, Δθ = 0)
Y — Vertical Fold     — phase progression                         (ΔF = 0, Δθ = π/3)
Z — Learning          — persistent structural change              (ΔL, carried forward)

Horizontal + Vertical + Learning  =  3D K'uhul state evolution

That gives K'uhul a three-coordinate state:

𝒦_t  =  (F_t, θ_t, L_t)

  F_t ⊆ G_semantic   — active semantic field   (X)
  θ_t ∈ G_phase      — current K'uhul phase    (Y)
  L_t                — accumulated learned structure  (Z)

Each transformation is a displacement along exactly one axis:

AxisOperationDisplacementScope
XHorizontal Fold(F_t, θ, L) → (F_{t+1}, θ, L)Current context window
YVertical Fold(F, θ_t, L) → (F, θ_t+π/3, L)Current context window
ZLearn(F, θ, L_t) → (F, θ, L_{t+1})Persistent — carried forward to all future states

When <learn> extends the semantic graph, the Z displacement is:

L_{t+1}  =  L_t + ΔL
G_semantic^(t+1)  =  G_semantic^(t) ⊕ ΔG

Horizontal and vertical movement occur within the existing state space. Z movement changes the structure that is carried forward — new nodes, new associations, new Book memberships, new future traversal possibilities.

Recursive 2π Accumulation

Each complete revolution through C₆ can leave a Z-axis deposit:

𝒦_0  →[2π]→  𝒦_1  →[2π]→  𝒦_2  →[2π]→  𝒦_3

Each cycle: X (field traversal) + Y (phase progression) → optional Z (structural deposit)

AIML's <learn> is the concrete instance: a category learned during one cycle creates new traversal possibilities in all subsequent cycles. The semantic graph at 𝒦_3 may be structurally richer than at 𝒦_0 — not because the X/Y axes changed, but because Z accumulated depth.

Scope Asymmetry and the Helix

The three axes have different persistence properties — X and Y describe where the current computation is; Z describes what previous computation has permanently changed:

AxisPersistenceMeaning
X — FieldContextual — resets with contextWhat is semantically active right now
Y — PhaseCyclical — resets modulo 2πWhere that information is in the K'uhul cycle
Z — LearningPersistent — survives into future cyclesWhat the system has learned across cycles

Because θ + 2π ≡ θ, the phase coordinate returns — Yn+6 = Yn. But the full K'uhul state does not necessarily return:

𝒦_{t+2π}  ≠  𝒦_t      when ΔL ≠ 0  (learning occurred)

Without learning:  ΔL = 0  →  𝒦_0 →[2π]→ 𝒦_0'   L_0' = L_0   (flat circle)
With learning:     ΔL ≠ 0  →  𝒦_0 →[2π]→ 𝒦_1 →[2π]→ 𝒦_2 →...  (helix)

Repeated phase revolutions without learning close on the same Z-plane. With learning, each revolution can occupy a new Z-depth:

      Z ↑        ╭────● cycle 3
        │      ╭─╯
        │    ╭────● cycle 2
        │  ╭─╯
        │╭────● cycle 1
        ●
       ╱ ╲
      X   Y  (2π cycle)

Z Changes the Space for Future X

Z does not merely record history. Learned structure alters Gsemantic, which changes what fields can be activated in future contexts — nodes unreachable before learning become reachable after:

Before:   L_t     →  G_semantic^(t)   →  F_Q^(t)
After:    L_{t+1} →  G_semantic^(t+1) →  F_Q^(t+1)

F_Q^(t+1) may contain nodes that F_Q^(t) could not activate.

The X-axis draw-boundary depends on the current Z-depth. Each Z-layer genuinely expands the reachable X-space for all subsequent cycles — this is what makes the helix formulation more than geometric.

Book Evolution and Coherence Change

Because Z modifies Gsemantic, Book membership and Book coherence are time-indexed. Before learning:

N_t(CAUSE)  =  { WHY, REASON }

After <learn> introduces CAUSE ↔ BECAUSE:

L_{t+1}  =  L_t + ΔL
G_semantic^(t+1)  =  G_semantic^(t) ⊕ ΔG

N_{t+1}(CAUSE)  =  { WHY, REASON, BECAUSE }

The same future query Q produces a different field — not because Q changed, but because the Book it activates changed:

F_Q^(t+1)  ≠  F_Q^(t)    for the same Q

If the new association closes a triangle (WHY↔REASON↔BECAUSE), the clustering coefficient changes too:

κ_{t+1}(CAUSE)  ≠  κ_t(CAUSE)

Learning changes not just what is in a Book, but how internally coherent that Book is.

Clean definition of learning: Learning changes the future semantic field produced by the same context.
K'uhul is cyclic in phase, contextual in field, and cumulative in learning
F × S¹ × L

F (X) — contextual: the active semantic field, resets with context
